The point estimator for the solution is given by60\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{f}^{\textrm{BG}} = R^T \left( W + \lambda C_Y\right) ^{-1} Y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f^BG=RTW+λCY-1Y,and the resolution function is given by61\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {R}^{\textrm{BG}}&= R^T \left( W + \lambda C_Y\right) ^{-1} (\textrm{FK})\end{aligned}$$\end{document}RBG=RTW+λCY-1(FK)62\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&= K (\textrm{FK})^T\left( (\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T+ \lambda C_Y\right) ^{-1} (\textrm{FK}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}=K(FK)T(FK)K(FK)T+λCY-1(FK).We have suppressed all the indices in the equations above. It may be useful to rewrite the resolution function with all the indices explicitly written as a way to get familiar with the notation. These expressions will be useful in connecting the BG result with other solutions that we will discuss in the next sections.

Closure tests

Closure tests are a powerful tool to validate the solutions of the inverse problem, and to assess the quality of the reconstructed function. The idea is to generate synthetic data from a known solution, and then to apply, in this particular case, the BG method to these data to see if we can recover the original solution. This allows us to check that the implementation of the BG method is correct, and to understand how well the method performs in practice. The synthetic data are generated by applying the forward operator to a known solution, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0, and adding noise to mimic the statistical uncertainty of the data. Using the grid notation introduced in Sect. 3.2, the synthetic data are generated as63\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y = (\textrm{FK})f_0 + \epsilon , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Y=(FK)f0+ϵ,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0 is the known solution, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textrm{FK})$$\end{document}(FK) is the forward operator, and εϵ is a vector of random variables with zero mean and covariance matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY. The BG method is then applied to the synthetic data Y to obtain an estimator \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{f}^{\textrm{BG}}$$\end{document}f^BG for the solution and compare it with the known solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0.

Writing explicitly all the matrices that appear in the BG solution in Eq. (60) yields64\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{f}^{\textrm{BG}}&= K (\textrm{FK})^T\left( (\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T+ \lambda C_Y\right) ^{-1} (\textrm{FK})f_0 \nonumber \\&\qquad + K (\textrm{FK})^T\left( (\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T+ \lambda C_Y\right) ^{-1} \epsilon . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f^BG=K(FK)T(FK)K(FK)T+λCY-1(FK)f0+K(FK)T(FK)K(FK)T+λCY-1ϵ.The first term on the right-hand side of the equation above is the contribution to the BG solution that comes from the known solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0, while the second term is the contribution that comes from the noise in the data. The second contribution is linear in the noise εϵ and therefore averages to zero over an ensemble of instances of the noise. The first contribution, as already discussed in Sect. 3.1, is a smeared version of the known solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0. A few algebraic manipulations allow us to rewrite the BG solution as65\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{f}^{\textrm{BG}}&= \mathcal {R}^{\textrm{BG}} f_0 = K M \frac{1}{K M + \lambda } f_0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}f^BG=RBGf0=KM1KM+λf0,where66\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M = (\textrm{FK})^TC_Y^{-1} (\textrm{FK}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}M=(FK)TCY-1(FK),is the matrix we already introduced in Eq. (27). If M were invertible, then we would recover the exact solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0 in the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}λ→0. As discussed in Sect. 2, the matrix M is not invertible, and the BG solution is one prescription to regularize the problem. In the case where M is invertible but ill-conditioned, the BG solution for nonvanishing λλ is a Tikhonov regularization of the problem. Equation (65) shows explicitly that the BG prescription yields a smeared version of the solution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0, where the smearing is dictated by the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K M \frac{1}{K M + \lambda }$$\end{document}KM1KM+λ.

Lattice spectral densities and the HLT method

A contemporary application of the BG method is in the determination of spectral densities from Euclidean correlators computed in lattice field theories. Here, we focus on the HLT method as introduced in Ref. [9], which is a particular implementation of the BG method, tailored to the specific problem of spectral density reconstruction. We begin by formulating the problem and introducing the notation that is specific to this application. We follow the notation used in the original publication, which is different from the one we used in the general formulation of the problem in Sects. 2.1, 3.1, and 3.2.

Observables.

The observables used to extract the spectral densities are Euclidean correlators of field operators, which are defined as67\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_L(t) = \frac{1}{L^3} \sum _{\vec {x}} \langle O(t,\vec {x}) O(0) \rangle _L, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CL(t)=1L3∑x→⟨O(t,x→)O(0)⟩L,where L is the spatial extent of the lattice, and t>0t>0 is the Euclidean time. The subscript L is a reminder of the fact that these correlators are computed in a finite volume. They are evaluated as the mean over an ensemble of gauge configurations generate by Monte Carlo sampling. Their covariance matrix is also computed from the available sample and scales like the inverse of the number of statistically independent configurations. The correlators are computed for a finite set of discrete time points t = a, 2a, … , Tt=a,2a,…,T, where a is the lattice spacing and T is the temporal extent of the lattice in physical units.4 The index I used to label the data points in the previous sections can be identified with the time index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_I$$\end{document}tI of the correlators, so that we can write \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_I = C_L(t_I)$$\end{document}YI=CL(tI).

The unknown function.

The function that we want to determine as the solution of the inverse problem is the spectral density68\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho _L(E) = \frac{1}{L^3} \sum _{\vec {x}} \langle O(0,\vec {x})\, \delta (E - H_L)\, O(0)\rangle _L, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρL(E)=1L3∑x→⟨O(0,x→)δ(E-HL)O(0)⟩L,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$H_L$$\end{document}HL is the Hamiltonian of the theory in a finite volume. The spectral density is a positive-definite function of the energy E, and it contains information about the spectrum of the theory, as well as about the matrix elements of the operator O between the vacuum and the energy eigenstates. Comparing again with the notation used in the previous sections for the general formulation of the inverse problem, we can identify the unknown function f(x) with the spectral density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho _L(E)$$\end{document}ρL(E). The argument of the unknown function in this case is the energy E; when we discretize the problem on a gird of points, the index αα is used to label the grid points with the energy variable \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_\alpha$$\end{document}Eα.

Formulation of the problem.

The Euclidean correlators, computed on a lattice with infinite time extent, and the spectral density are related by a Laplace transform69\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_L(t) = \int _0^\infty \textrm{d}E\, e^{-tE} \rho _L(E). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CL(t)=∫0∞dEe-tEρL(E).Note that in a finite volume, the spectrum of the Hamiltonian is discrete, which implies that the spectral density is a sum of delta functions70\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho _L(E) = \sum _{n} \rho _n(L) \delta (E - E_n), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρL(E)=∑nρn(L)δ(E-En),and therefore, it is a distribution rather than a regular function. As usual when dealing with distributions, we need to introduce a smearing function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _\sigma (E,E')$$\end{document}Δσ(E,E′) in order to define a regular, smeared version of the spectral density71\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_L(\sigma ,E_*) = \int _0^\infty \textrm{d}E \, \Delta _\sigma (E,E_*) \rho _L(E). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^L(σ,E∗)=∫0∞dEΔσ(E,E∗)ρL(E).The smearing function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _\sigma (E,E')$$\end{document}Δσ(E,E′) is a regular function, which is peaked around E = E'E=E′ and has a width σσ. The infinite-volume, unsmeared spectral density can be obtained by taking the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}σ→0 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$L \rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}L→∞ in the smeared spectral density72\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho (E) = \lim _{\sigma \rightarrow 0} \lim _{L \rightarrow \infty } \hat{\rho }_L(\sigma ,E), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ(E)=limσ→0limL→∞ρ^L(σ,E),where the order of the limits matters.

Note that the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}σ→0 may not be needed in practice, since the smeared spectral density \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_L(\sigma ,E)$$\end{document}ρ^L(σ,E) is a regular function of E that can be directly compared with experimental data, which can also be smeared.

HLT formalism on a finite lattice.

For correlators computed on a lattice with finite time extent T, the relation between the correlators and the spectral density is given by73\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y_I = C_L(t_I) = \int _0^\infty \textrm{d}E\, b_T(t_I,E) \rho _L(E), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}YI=CL(tI)=∫0∞dEbT(tI,E)ρL(E),where the basis function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_T(t,E)$$\end{document}bT(t,E) is given by74\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} b_T(t,E) = e^{-tE} + e^{-(T-t)E}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}bT(t,E)=e-tE+e-(T-t)E.The basis function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_T(t,E)$$\end{document}bT(t,E) is a generalization of the Laplace kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{-tE}$$\end{document}e-tE that takes into account the finite time extent of the lattice. In the limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T \rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}T→∞, the basis function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_T(t,E)$$\end{document}bT(t,E) reduces to the Laplace kernel75\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \lim _{T \rightarrow \infty } b_T(t,E) = e^{-tE} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}limT→∞bT(t,E)=e-tE.The function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_T(t_I,E)$$\end{document}bT(tI,E) is plays the role of the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_I(x)$$\end{document}CI(x) that we introduced in the general formulation of the inverse problem—remember that I identifies the data point and E is the argument of the unknown function.

HLT solution.

In the HLT formulation, as in all BG formulations, the solution is expanded as a linear combination of the data76\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{\rho }_{L}(E^*) = \sum _{t=0}^{t_{\textrm{max}}} g_t(E^*)\, C(t+a), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρ^L(E∗)=∑t=0tmaxgt(E∗)C(t+a),so that the inverse problem is reduced to determining the coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_t(E^*)$$\end{document}gt(E∗). As discussed in Sect. 3.1, any BG estimator yields a smeared version of the unknown function. In the notation of HLT, the smearing kernel is77\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \bar{\Delta }(E^*,E) = \sum _{t=0}^{t_{\textrm{max}}} g_t(E^*)\, b_T(t+a, E). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Δ¯(E∗,E)=∑t=0tmaxgt(E∗)bT(t+a,E).The coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_t(E^*)$$\end{document}gt(E∗) are determined by requiring that the smearing kernel approximates a specific function, e.g.78\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \Delta _{\sigma }(E^*,E) = \frac{1}{\mathcal {N}} \, \exp \left[ - \frac{\left( E - E^*\right) ^2}{2\sigma ^2} \right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Δσ(E∗,E)=1Nexp-E-E∗22σ2,with the unusual normalization79\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {N} = \int _{0}^{\infty } \textrm{d}E\, \exp \left[ - \frac{\left( E - E^*\right) ^2}{2\sigma ^2} \right] . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}N=∫0∞dEexp-E-E∗22σ2.Following the derivation in Sect. 3.1, for each value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^*$$\end{document}E∗, we minimize a cost function W[λ ,g]W[λ,g] with respect to the parameters \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_t(E^*)$$\end{document}gt(E∗). For each value of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E^*$$\end{document}E∗, we thereby determine \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(t_{\textrm{max}} + 1)$$\end{document}(tmax+1) coefficients, which are necessary to construct \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{\rho }_{L}(E^*)$$\end{document}ρ^L(E∗) according to Eq. 76. which is the sum of the distance between \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _{\sigma }$$\end{document}Δσ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\bar{\Delta }$$\end{document}Δ¯ and a term that takes into account the covariance of the data80\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W\left[ \lambda , g\right] = \left( 1-\lambda \right) A[g] + \lambda \frac{B[g]}{C(0)^2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Wλ,g=1-λA[g]+λB[g]C(0)2,where81\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A[g]&= \int _{E_0}^{\infty } \textrm{d}E\, \left| \Delta _{\sigma }(E^*,E) - \bar{\Delta }(E^*,E) \right| ^2\, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A[g]=∫E0∞dEΔσ(E∗,E)-Δ¯(E∗,E)282\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} B[g]&= \sum _{t,t'} g_t(E^*) \left( C_Y^{-1}\right) _{tt'} g_{t'}(E^*), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}B[g]=∑t,t′gt(E∗)CY-1tt′gt′(E∗),and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY is the covariance of the Euclidean correlators83\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left( C_Y\right) _{tt'} = \textrm{Cov}[C(t), C(t')]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CYtt′=Cov[C(t),C(t′)].Note that the integral in Eq. (81) has a lower integration limit \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E_0$$\end{document}E0, which cuts off the small values of E.

The two terms, A[g] and B[g], are the equivalent of the two terms that appear in Eq. 50; in the case of HLT, the coefficients are chosen to approximate a well-behaved smearing kernel rather than the singular delta distribution of Eq. (50). The main idea is that the HLT solution aims directly for a smeared solution, with the emphasis being on approximating the smearing kernel as best as possible, rather than trying to approximate a Dirac delta.

Exercise 3

Show that the solution of the minimization yields84\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} g^{\textrm{HLT}}_t(E^*; \lambda , \sigma )&= W^{-1}(\lambda )_{tt'} \left[ f_{t'}(E^*; \lambda , \sigma ) + R_{t'} \frac{1 - R^T W^{-1}(\lambda ) f(E^*; \lambda ,\sigma )}{R^T W^{-1}(\lambda ) R} \right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}gtHLT(E∗;λ,σ)=W-1(λ)tt′ft′(E∗;λ,σ)+Rt′1-RTW-1(λ)f(E∗;λ,σ)RTW-1(λ)R,where we introduced85\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&R_t = \int _0^\infty \textrm{d}E\, b_T(t+a, E), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Rt=∫0∞dEbT(t+a,E),86\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&f_t(E^*; \lambda , \sigma ) = \left( 1 - \lambda \right) \, \int _{E_0}^\infty \textrm{d}E\, b_t(t+a, E) \Delta _{\sigma }(E^*, E), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ft(E∗;λ,σ)=1-λ∫E0∞dEbt(t+a,E)Δσ(E∗,E),and87\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W(\lambda )_{tt'} = (1-\lambda ) M_{tt'} + \lambda \frac{(C_Y)_{tt'}}{C(0)^2}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}W(λ)tt′=(1-λ)Mtt′+λ(CY)tt′C(0)2,where88\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} M_{tt'} = \int _{E_0}^\infty \textrm{d}E\, b_T(t+a, E)\, b_T(t'+a, E). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Mtt′=∫E0∞dEbT(t+a,E)bT(t′+a,E).The matrix M is an ill-conditioned matrix; as discussed in Sect. 3.1 adding to the distance the B term, which takes into account the covariance of the data, acts as a regulator.

A comment on A.

For \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T\rightarrow \infty$$\end{document}T→∞, the function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_T$$\end{document}bT becomes an exponential, and therefore, the A term in the distance is89\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} A[g] = \int _{E_0}^\infty \textrm{d}E\, \left| \sum _{t=0}^{t_{\textrm{max}}} g_t(E^*;\lambda , \sigma ) e^{-(t+a) E} - \Delta _{\sigma }(E^*,E) \right| ^2. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}A[g]=∫E0∞dE∑t=0tmaxgt(E∗;λ,σ)e-(t+a)E-Δσ(E∗,E)2.

Some insight into the HLT solution.

The HLT approach focuses on approximating the smearing kernel \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Delta _{\sigma }(E^*,E)$$\end{document}Δσ(E∗,E) as best as possible, rather than trying to find the best point estimator for the spectral density. It is possible, and desirable, to monitor the quality of the approximation of the smearing kernel using as an estimator90\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta _{\sigma }(E^*, E) = 1 - \frac{\bar{\Delta }(E^*,E)}{\Delta _\sigma (E^*,E)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δσ(E∗,E)=1-Δ¯(E∗,E)Δσ(E∗,E).The quality of the approximation of the smearing kernel can be improved by increasing the number of data points, i.e., by increasing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_{\textrm{max}}$$\end{document}tmax. It is worthwhile to emphasize the quantitative character of this estimate; for a given quality of the kernel approximation, e.g., fixing91\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \delta _{\sigma }(E^*, E^*) = 0.05, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}δσ(E∗,E∗)=0.05,allows to explore regions in the σσ vs. \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_{\textrm{max}}$$\end{document}tmax plane where the approximation of the smearing kernel is good enough, and therefore to determine the optimal choice of σσ for a given number of data points.

However, the quality of the approximation is also affected by the choice of the regularization parameter λλ. Increasing λλ improves the stability of the solution with respect to noise in the data, but it also worsens the approximation of the smearing kernel. The optimal choice of λλ is, therefore, a trade-off between the quality of the approximation of the smearing kernel and the stability of the solution with respect to noise in the data.

Gaussian processes

A brief introduction to Gaussian processes

A stochastic process is a collection of random variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{f(x): x \in \mathcal {I}\}$$\end{document}{f(x):x∈I}, where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {I}$$\end{document}I is the set on which f is defined. For any discrete set of points \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{x_1, \ldots , x_n\}$$\end{document}{x1,…,xn}, the random variables \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\{f(x_1), \ldots , f(x_n)\}$$\end{document}{f(x1),…,f(xn)} have a joint probability distribution. A Gaussian process is a stochastic process, such that any finite collection of random variables has a multivariate Gaussian distribution. As such, a Gaussian process is completely specified by its mean function m(x) and its covariance function k(x,x')k(x,x′), defined as92\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m(x) = \mathbb {E}[f(x)], \qquad k(x,x') = \mathbb {E}[(f(x) - m(x))(f(x') - m(x'))]. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m(x)=E[f(x)],k(x,x′)=E[(f(x)-m(x))(f(x′)-m(x′))].In the context of the inverse problems discussed in these lectures, we use Gaussian processes to define a prior distribution over functions and then use the observed data to obtain a posterior distribution, as suggested in Sect. 2.3.

Setup of the problem.

Following the conventions in Sect. 2.2, we represent the function f as an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\textrm{grid}}$$\end{document}Ngrid-dimensional real vector, made of the values of the function f at selected points on a grid:93\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} f_{\alpha } = f(x_{\alpha })\, \quad \alpha = 1, \ldots , N_{\textrm{grid}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}fα=f(xα)α=1,…,Ngrid.These values are promoted to stochastic variables with a joint prior probability distribution that is a multi-dimensional Gaussian with respective mean and covariance given by94\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} m_{\alpha }&= m(x_{\alpha }) = \mathbb {E}_{P}[f_{\alpha }], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}mα=m(xα)=EP[fα],95\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K_{\alpha _1\alpha _2}&= k(x_{\alpha _1},x_{\alpha _2}) = \textrm{Cov}_{P}[f_{\alpha _1},f_{\alpha _2}], \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Kα1α2=k(xα1,xα2)=CovP[fα1,fα2],where we introduced the subscript P to remind the reader that here we consider averages over the prior distribution P96\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(f | h) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\det \left( 2\pi K\right) }} \exp \left\{ - \frac{1}{2} \left( f-m\right) ^T K^{-1} \left( f - m\right) \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(f|h)=1det2πKexp-12f-mTK-1f-m.The covariance matrix of the Gaussian prior in this section is denoted by K, a symbol we used in Sect. 3 to denote the metric in the space of functions. The reason for this choice is that the covariance of the prior distribution plays a similar role to the metric in the Backus–Gilbert method.

Any parameter that appears in the definitions of m and k is called an hyperparameter and is collectively denoted by h. For the PDF determinations that we are going to analyze later, we choose m(x) = 0m(x)=0 and a Gibbs kernel97\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} k(x,x') = \sigma ^2 \sqrt{\frac{2 \ell (x) \ell (x')}{\ell (x)^2 + \ell (x')^2}}\, \exp \left[ - \frac{(x-x')^2}{\ell (x)^2 + \ell (x')^2} \right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}k(x,x′)=σ22ℓ(x)ℓ(x′)ℓ(x)2+ℓ(x′)2exp-(x-x′)2ℓ(x)2+ℓ(x′)2,where98\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \ell (x) = \ell _0 (x + \delta ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ℓ(x)=ℓ0(x+δ).There are two hyperparameters in this kernel, σσ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _0$$\end{document}ℓ0, while δδ is a small regulator, which is introduced in order to avoid the singularity at x=0x=0. By inspecting Eq. (97), we see that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma ^2$$\end{document}σ2 is the variance of f(x), which is the same for all values of x. This is a choice that is encoded in the prior. Other choices are possible, including the possibility of an x-dependent prefactor. The correlation between two values f(x) and f(x')f(x′) decreases exponentially with the square of the distance between x and x'x′. The rate of the exponential decay is dictated by ℓ (x)ℓ(x) and ℓ (x')ℓ(x′). For this particular choice of the prior, ℓ (x)ℓ(x) grows linearly with x, which implies that the correlation between points decreases as x→ 0x→0. The values of f for nearby points at small x are almost uncorrelated, which implies that the function is free to fluctuate more than at higher values of x. We will discuss later how to constrain the hyperparameters σσ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\ell _0$$\end{document}ℓ0.

Remembering that the theoretical predictions are computed by multiplying the vector f by the forward map \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textrm{FK})$$\end{document}(FK)99\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T = (\textrm{FK})f, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T=(FK)f,it is clear that these are also Gaussian variables, and one can readily show that the average and the covariance of the theoretical prediction T, induced by the prior probability distribution of f, are given by100\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} E[T_I]&= (\textrm{FK})_{I\alpha } m_\alpha , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[TI]=(FK)Iαmα,101\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \textrm{Cov}[T_I,T_J]&= (\textrm{FK})_{I\alpha _1} K_{\alpha _1\alpha _2} (\textrm{FK})^T_{\alpha _1J}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Cov[TI,TJ]=(FK)Iα1Kα1α2(FK)α1JT.Clearly, T is a vector of dimensions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\textrm{dat}}$$\end{document}Ndat, with components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_I$$\end{document}TI, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$I = 1, \ldots , N_{\textrm{dat}}$$\end{document}I=1,…,Ndat—there is one theoretical prediction for every data point.

Finally, we have \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\textrm{dat}}$$\end{document}Ndat data points. Their central values, as given by the experiments, are denoted \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Y_I$$\end{document}YI, while their fluctuations are assumed to be Gaussian, with a covariance matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY, which also taken from experimental analyses. The fluctuations of the data around the central values are described by a stochastic variable102\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \epsilon \sim \mathcal {N}\left( 0, C_Y\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϵ∼N0,CY.Remember once again that εϵ is an \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{\textrm{dat}}$$\end{document}Ndat-dimensional vector, with components \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _I$$\end{document}ϵI. The set of central values and the covariance matrix of the data together constitute what we call the dataset \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}}$$\end{document}DNdat.

The Gaussian process solution

The solution of the inverse problem in this formalism is encoded in the joint posterior distribution103\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{P}(f, h) = P\left( f, h | D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P~(f,h)=Pf,h|DNdat.Using simple probabilities, we can factorize104\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P\left( f, h | D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}}\right) = P(f | h, D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}})\, P(h | D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}}); \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pf,h|DNdat=P(f|h,DNdat)P(h|DNdat);the joint posterior is the product of two factors. The first one is the posterior probability induced by the data at fixed values of the hyperparameters h, while the second factor is the probability of the hyperparameters given the data. Let us analyze each factor on the right-hand side in turn.

The first factor is computed using Bayesian inference105\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(f | h, D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}}) = \frac{P(D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}} | f, h)\, P(f | h)}{P(D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}} | h)}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(f|h,DNdat)=P(DNdat|f,h)P(f|h)P(DNdat|h).The denominator in Eq. (105) is just a normalization factor, independent of f, which can be computed a posteriori. We focus here on on the expression in the numerator, which is the product of two factors, the likelihood and the prior. The likelihood is the probability of the data given the function f and the hyperparameters h. Assuming that the data are normally distributed around the theoretical predictions, we have106\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}} | f, h) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\det \left( 2\pi C_Y\right) }} \exp \left\{ - \frac{1}{2} \left( Y - (\textrm{FK})f\right) ^T C_Y^{-1} \left( Y - (\textrm{FK})f\right) \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(DNdat|f,h)=1det2πCYexp-12Y-(FK)fTCY-1Y-(FK)f.For linear data, i.e. for theoretical predictions that depend linearly on the function f, the likelihood is a Gaussian distribution in f. The prior is also a Gaussian distribution in f, as given by Eq. (96). The product of two Gaussian distributions is another Gaussian distribution, so that the posterior distribution at fixed hyperparameters is also a Gaussian distribution in f. The mean and the covariance of this posterior distribution can be computed analytically, and are given by107\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{m}&= m + K (\textrm{FK})^T\left( C_{YT}\right) ^{-1} (Y - (\textrm{FK})m), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m~=m+K(FK)TCYT-1(Y-(FK)m),108\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{K}&= K - K (\textrm{FK})^T\left( C_{YT}\right) ^{-1} (\textrm{FK})K, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K~=K-K(FK)TCYT-1(FK)K,where we introduced the matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{YT}$$\end{document}CYT109\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} C_{YT} = C_Y + (\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}CYT=CY+(FK)K(FK)T.Note that, because \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{YT}$$\end{document}CYT is positive-definite, the diagonal elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{K}$$\end{document}K~ are smaller than the diagonal elements of K. Recalling that the diagonal elements of K and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{K}$$\end{document}K~ are, respectively, the variances of the prior and posterior distributions, we see that the that Bayesian inference reduced the uncertainty on the function f at fixed hyperparameters. The information contained in the data leads to a reduction of the uncertainty on the function f.

It is useful to prove that110\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} (\textrm{FK})^T\left[ C_Y + (\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T\right] ^{-1} = \frac{1}{1 + M K} (\textrm{FK})^TC_Y^{-1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}(FK)TCY+(FK)K(FK)T-1=11+MK(FK)TCY-1,with the usual definition of the matrix M in Eq. (27), and therefore note that the covariance of the posterior can be rewritten in a form that is more similar to the expression for the covariance of the BG solution111\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{K}^{-1} = K^{-1} + M. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K~-1=K-1+M.This expression makes it clear that the posterior covariance is the result of the combination of the prior covariance and the information contained in the data, as encoded in M. The posterior covariance is smaller than both the prior covariance and the covariance of the BG solution, which is given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$M^{-1}$$\end{document}M-1, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{K}^{-1}> K^{-1}$$\end{document}K~-1>K-1 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\tilde{K}^{-1}> M$$\end{document}K~-1>M.

Exercise 4

Prove Eqs. (107), (108), (110), and (111).

Using Eq. (110), the mean of the posterior distribution can be rewritten as112\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{m} = m + K \frac{1}{1 + M K} (\textrm{FK})^TC_Y^{-1} (Y - (\textrm{FK})m). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m~=m+K11+MK(FK)TCY-1(Y-(FK)m).This expression makes it clear that the posterior mean is the result of the combination of the prior mean and the information contained in the data, with a corrections that is proportional to the difference between the data and the theoretical predictions computed using the prior mean. For m=0m=0, we can rewrite the posterior mean as113\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{m} = \left[ K \frac{1}{1 + M K}\right] \, (\textrm{FK})^TC_Y^{-1} Y. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m~=K11+MK(FK)TCY-1Y.

A note on Tikhonov regularization.

By choosing a prior covariance of the form \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K = \sigma ^2 I$$\end{document}K=σ2I, where λλ is a positive parameter, we can rewrite the posterior mean as114\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{m} = \frac{1}{M + \lambda }\, (\textrm{FK})^TC_Y^{-1} Y, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m~=1M+λ(FK)TCY-1Y,which is the Tikhonov regularized solution of the inverse problem, with regularization parameter \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda = 1/\sigma ^2$$\end{document}λ=1/σ2. The Tikhonov regularized solution is a particular case of the GP solution, characterized by a diagonal prior covariance, i.e. a prior that does not encode any correlation between the values of the function at different points.

The second factor in Eq. (104) is the probability of the hyperparameters given the data. This is computed using Bayes’ theorem as well115\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(h | D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}}) = \frac{P(D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}} | h)\, P(h)}{P(D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}})}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(h|DNdat)=P(DNdat|h)P(h)P(DNdat).Once again, we only need to focus on the numerator, since the denominator is just a normalization factor. At fixed values of the hyperparameters, the data can be modeled as116\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y = (\textrm{FK})f + \epsilon , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Y=(FK)f+ϵ,where f is a stochastic variable with distribution given by the prior, and εϵ is a stochastic variable with distribution given by Eq. (102). Therefore, the data are the sum of two Gaussian variables, and are itself a Gaussian variable117\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P(D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}} | h) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{\det \left( 2\pi C_{YT}\right) }} \exp \left\{ - \frac{1}{2} \left( Y - (\textrm{FK})m\right) ^T C_{YT}^{-1} \left( Y - (\textrm{FK})m\right) \right\} . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}P(DNdat|h)=1det2πCYTexp-12Y-(FK)mTCYT-1Y-(FK)m.Choosing a uniform prior for the hyperparameters, the posterior distribution of the hyperparameters is proportional to the likelihood in Eq. (117). The most likely values of the hyperparameters are those that maximize this likelihood. Note that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P(D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}} | h)$$\end{document}P(DNdat|h) is a complicated function of the hyperparameters, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_{YT}$$\end{document}CYT depends on h in a non-trivial way. However, the distribution can be sampled using standard Markov Chain Monte Carlo methods, which allow us to obtain a characterization of the posterior distribution of the hyperparameters. If the posterior distribution of the hyperparameters is sharply peaked around its maximum, we can approximate it with a delta function, and use the most likely values of the hyperparameters in Eqs. (107) and (108) to obtain an approximation of the posterior distribution of f.

GP determination of one PDF

A simple application of the GP methodology is the determination of the PDF118\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_3(x) = u(x) + \bar{u}(x) - d(x) - \bar{d}(x), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}T3(x)=u(x)+u¯(x)-d(x)-d¯(x),using DIS data from the BCDMS experiment. The difference between the proton and the deuteron structure functions, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$F_2^p - F_2^d$$\end{document}F2p-F2d, is proportional to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3 only, so that we can write119\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} F_2^p(x,Q^2) - F_2^d(x,Q2) = C_{T_3} \otimes T_3(x). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}F2p(x,Q2)-F2d(x,Q2)=CT3⊗T3(x).This is a simple example of an inverse problem in the realm of PDFs determinations, where only one PDF is involved, and the forward map is given by a convolution with a kernel that is known in perturbation theory. The solution of this problem can be obtained using the GP methodology described in the previous section.

All results presented here are obtained using synthetic data generated from a known solution. The approach follows very closely the results presented in Ref. [10]. After applying standard kinematic cuts, we have 248 data points for the structure functions with their corresponding covariance matrix, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY, as described by the experimental analyses [11]. In the case of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3, we want to implement the fact that the function has to be integrable at x=0x=0. This can be achieved by introducing and additional hyperparameter αα, used to rescale the kernel as120\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} k(x,x') \rightarrow \phi (x) \, k(x,x') \, \phi (x'), \quad \phi (x) = x^\alpha , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}k(x,x′)→ϕ(x)k(x,x′)ϕ(x′),ϕ(x)=xα,and imposing \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\alpha \in (-1,0]$$\end{document}α∈(-1,0].

The Bayesian inference is performed in two steps. First, we sample the posterior of the hyperparameters, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P(h | D_{N_{\textrm{dat}}})$$\end{document}P(h|DNdat), using Markov Chain Monte Carlo methods. Then, for each set of hyperparameters drawn from this posterior, we use the analytical expressions in Eqs. (107) and (108) and generate a replica of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3. The ensemble of replicas generated in this way provides a characterization of the posterior distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3, which can be used to compute the mean and the uncertainty of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3 at any value of x. Results for the posterior distribution of the hyperparameters are shown in Fig. 1.Fig. 11-dimensional (left panel) and 2-dimensional (right panel) posteriors of the hyperparameters αα, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_0$$\end{document}l0 and σσ. The hyperparameter σσ is characterized by a sharply peaked posterior located around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\sigma \sim 0.25$$\end{document}σ∼0.25 which quickly decays to zero (for this reason in the posterior plot only the region \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( 0,1\right)$$\end{document}0,1 is shown, even if the support of the prior is \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\left( 0,10\right)$$\end{document}0,10); αα tends to sit closer to 0, with a slow decay for smaller values toward -1-1; the \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_0$$\end{document}l0 posterior discards the smaller values of the correlation length, it shows a peak for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$l_0\sim 1.7$$\end{document}l0∼1.7 and then remains fairly constant. (Plot taken from Ref. [10])

(Plot taken from Ref. [10])

The mean value and the uncertainty of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3 obtained from the posterior distribution are shown in Fig. 2. The mean value is taken by averaging over the ensemble of replicas at each value of x, while the uncertainties are obtained from the 68% and 95% confidence intervals of the distribution of replicas. The uncertainty due to the fluctuations of the hyperparameters is included in the final uncertainty of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3, since the replicas are generated by sampling the posterior distribution of the hyperparameters and then using the normal distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3 at fixed hyperparameters. The Bayesian inference is able to reconstruct the functional form of the PDF, as can be seen by comparing the dark blue and the dotted lines in the figure, which represent the mean of the posterior distribution and the input PDF, respectively. The uncertainty on \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3 is smaller in the regions where the data are more constraining, and increases in the extrapolation regions at small and large x, where the results are mostly determined by the choice of the prior.Fig. 2Samples from the posterior distribution of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T_3$$\end{document}T3, plotted in linear (left panel) and log (right panel) scale. The shaded regions represent the 68CL and 95CL intervals, and in light blue, we plot a few representative samples from the distribution. The posterior displays a smaller variance in the regions sensitive to experimental data, and an increasing spread in the small and large-x extrapolation regions, where the results are mostly determined by the chosen prior. (Plot taken from Ref. [10])
