Closure tests

Following the discussion in Sect. 3.3, we can perform closure tests to validate the implementation of the GP method, and to assess the quality of the solution. Ignoring statistical fluctuations, i.e. setting \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y=0$$\end{document}CY=0, synthetic data are generated from a known solution, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0, by applying the forward operator121\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} Y = (\textrm{FK})f_0. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Y=(FK)f0.In this case, the posterior mean is given by122\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \tilde{m} = K (\textrm{FK})^T\left[ (\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T\right] ^{+} (\textrm{FK})\, f_0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m~=K(FK)T(FK)K(FK)T+(FK)f0,where the superscript + denotes the pseudo-inverse, which is needed, since \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$(\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T$$\end{document}(FK)K(FK)T is not invertible when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y=0$$\end{document}CY=0. By comparing with Eq. (64), we see that this solution corresponds to the solution obtained using the BG method with the kernel K as the metric. The two solutions coincide in the limit of zero noise when the hyperparameters are fixed to the values used to generate the data. It is interesting to note that, even when there is no statistical noise, the solution of the inverse problem does not coincide with the input function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0, but it is a smeared version of it, with the same smearing kernel given by the resolution function of the BG method. The solution of the inverse problem is not exact, even in the absence of noise, because of the smoothing effect of the forward map and the regularization effect of the prior. The resolution function in this case is given by123\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {R}^{\textrm{GP}}(x,x') = K(x,x') (\textrm{FK})^T\left[ (\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T\right] ^{+} (\textrm{FK}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}RGP(x,x′)=K(x,x′)(FK)T(FK)K(FK)T+(FK).A nonvanishing experimental error can be readily added to the closure test by adding a noise term to the data, which is normally distributed around zero, with a covariance given by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY. In this case, the mean of the posterior distribution is given by Eq. (107) and the reconstruction kernel is124\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {R} = K\, (\textrm{FK})^T\left[ (\textrm{FK})K (\textrm{FK})^T+ C_Y\right] ^{-1} (\textrm{FK}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R=K(FK)T(FK)K(FK)T+CY-1(FK),so that the posterior mean and covariance can be rewritten as125\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\tilde{m} - f_0 = \left[ \mathcal {R} - 1\!\!1\right] f_0 + a^T \epsilon , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}m~-f0=R-1f0+aTϵ,126\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\tilde{K} = \left( 1\!\!1 - \mathcal {R}\right) K \left( 1\!\!1 - \mathcal {R}\right) ^T + a^T C_Y a, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K~=1-RK1-RT+aTCYa,where we have introduced127\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} a^T = K\,(\textrm{FK})^T\left[ (\textrm{FK})\, K \, (\textrm{FK})^T+ C_Y\right] ^{-1}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}aT=K(FK)T(FK)K(FK)T+CY-1,so that128\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {R} = a^T (\textrm{FK}). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}R=aT(FK).The decomposition in Eqs. (125), (126) highlights the fact that there are two types of contributions to the bias and to the posterior covariance matrix. The first term in both Eqs. (125) and (126) comes from the limited reconstruction of the central value and indeed would vanish when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R}=1\!\!1$$\end{document}R=1. If \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R} \ne 1\!\!1$$\end{document}R≠1, this term survives in the limit where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y \rightarrow 0$$\end{document}CY→0, i.e. in the limit of no experimental errors on the data. The second term is the propagation of the covariance of the data into the covariance of the model. In the case \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {R} = 1\!\!1$$\end{document}R=1, the only error fluctuations in the posterior distribution come from this term. In Fig. 3, Monte Carlo samples generated according to the reconstruction and experimental components are plotted separately for the BCDMS results, in red and gray, respectively. In the medium-x region, where more experimental data are available, the PDF uncertainty is dominated by the experimental error, yet a smaller reconstruction error is still present; when moving to the small and large-x extrapolation regions, the reconstruction error becomes the dominant one, pointing out the lack of experimental information. We stress once more how these qualitative considerations are precisely quantified in Eqs. (125), (126), giving the analytical expression for the posterior covariance matrices associated with the experimental and reconstruction error, making it possible to quote different component of the PDF error in the context of a phenomenology analysis.Fig. 3GP reconstruction of the input function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0, with the 68% CL band for the experimental and reconstruction errors plotted separately in gray and red, respectively, according to Eq. (126). The error bands are overlapped rather than stacked. Plot taken from Ref. [10]

GP reconstruction of the input function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_0$$\end{document}f0, with the 68% CL band for the experimental and reconstruction errors plotted separately in gray and red, respectively, according to Eq. Note that in this formalism, it is straightforward to assess the impact of a reduced uncertainty on the data, simply by reducing the covariance matrix \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY and recomputing the posterior distribution.

GP for spectral densities

Let us now discuss how to apply the GP formalism to the problem of reconstructing spectral densities from Euclidean correlators computed on the lattice. The notation in this section follows very closely the one used in Ref. [12].

GP solution and connection with BG

Following the formalism introduced in this section, we represent the spectral density as a stochastic field \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho (\omega )$$\end{document}ρ(ω), which is described by a GP centered around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{\textrm{prior}}(\omega )$$\end{document}ρprior(ω) and with covariance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}^{\textrm{prior}}(\omega ,E)$$\end{document}Kprior(ω,E). Exercise 5

Prove Eq. (142).

The posterior Gaussian distribution for the spectral density is centered around143\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left. \rho ^{\textrm{post}}(\omega ) \right| _{\mathcal {C}=C^{\textrm{obs}}} = \rho ^{\textrm{prior}}(\omega ) + \vec {F}^T(\omega ) \frac{1}{\Sigma + C_Y} \left( \vec {C}^{\;\mathrm obs} - \vec {C}^{\; \mathrm{prior}} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρpost(ω)C=Cobs=ρprior(ω)+F→T(ω)1Σ+CYC→obs-C→prior,and has variance144\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \left. \mathcal {K}^\textrm{post}(\omega ,\omega ) \right| _{\mathcal {C}=C^{\textrm{obs}}} = \mathcal {K}^{\textrm{prior}}(\omega ,\omega ) - \vec {F}^T(\omega ) \frac{1}{\Sigma + C_Y} \vec {F}(\omega ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Kpost(ω,ω)C=Cobs=Kprior(ω,ω)-F→T(ω)1Σ+CYF→(ω).Note that the posterior mean obtained in Eq. 144 is a linear combination of the data, as in the Backus–Gilbert method discussed in the previous section. In order to explicitly make contact with the BG result that we obtained in the previous section, we introduce the coefficients145\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vec {g}^{\mathrm{\, GP}} (\omega ) = \vec {F}^T \frac{1}{\Sigma + C_Y}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g→GP(ω)=F→T1Σ+CY,where the superscript GP reminds us that these are the coefficients obtained using the GP method.

Equation (145) shows that the problem is regularized by formulating it in terms of probability distributions. The numerical instability of the BG solution, which manifests itself in the very large coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\vec {g}^{\mathrm{\, GP}} (\omega )$$\end{document}g→GP(ω), is the consequence of the large condition number of the model covariance ΣΣ of Eq. (136), which grows exponentially for acceptable choices of the model covariance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}$$\end{document}K. In the absence of errors on the data, the magnitude of the coefficients is determined by the inverse of the matrix ΣΣ. For noisy data, the covariance \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY is added to the matrix ΣΣ, thereby providing a cut-off for its lower modes. The magnitude of the coefficients is now determined by the condition number of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma + C_Y$$\end{document}Σ+CY. We will later show how the choice of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {K}^{\textrm{prior}}$$\end{document}Kprior can be used to tune the cut-off on the low modes of ΣΣ.

Another important remark concerns the smearing kernel that one has implicitly introduced in Eq. (143)146\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {S}^{\textrm{GP}}(\omega ,E) = \sum _{\tau =1}^{\tau _{\textrm{max}}} \vec {g}^{\mathrm{\, GP}} (\omega ) \,b_T(a\tau ,E). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}SGP(ω,E)=∑τ=1τmaxg→GP(ω)bT(aτ,E).The center of the posterior distribution, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{\textrm{post}}(\omega )$$\end{document}ρpost(ω), is an estimator for a spectral density that is smeared with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}^{\textrm{GP}}$$\end{document}SGP. The unsmeared spectral density can be obtained if the prior is engineered, such that \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {S}^\textrm{GP}(\omega -E)$$\end{document}SGP(ω-E) is a Dirac delta. Such a distribution cannot be obtained by a finite linear combination of the regular functions \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b_T(t,E)$$\end{document}bT(t,E), and one would need to extrapolate the result at vanishing smearing radius.

We conclude this section with a crucial remark on the nature of the prior. The functional behavior of the prediction is related to the prior through Eqs. (143) and (144). In particular, the covariance of the prior is known to affect the typical correlation length of the posterior. On the lattice, finite-volume spectral densities are targeted; therefore, this length should be smaller than the typical spacing between energy levels in order to capture the features of the underlying physics.

Choice of the priors

The prior model covariance used in this lectures is a variation of a Gaussian covariance147\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {K}(\omega ,E) = \frac{e^{\alpha E}}{\lambda } \frac{e^{-\frac{(\omega -E)^2}{2\epsilon ^2}}}{\sqrt{2\pi } \epsilon } \equiv \frac{e^{\alpha E}}{\lambda } G_\epsilon (\omega -E), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}K(ω,E)=eαEλe-(ω-E)22ϵ22πϵ≡eαEλGϵ(ω-E),where εϵ, αα, and λλ are the hyperparameters that fully specify the prior. The motivations behind the choice made in Eq. (147) are the following. First, a Gaussian is a common choice in the literature [8, 13], with width εϵ and amplitude \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\lambda ^{-1}$$\end{document}λ-1 as parameters to be chosen. In addition, the Gaussian distribution has a simple limit in which the covariance becomes diagonal by changing the parameter εϵ. The term \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$e^{\alpha E}$$\end{document}eαE allows us to control the deviations from the Gaussian case, and provides a link with the results obtained using the method of Ref. [9]. In order to set the notation, we specialize the previous equations to this choice of the prior148\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \frac{\Sigma ^\epsilon _{tr}}{\lambda } = \int \textrm{d}E_1\, \textrm{d}E_2\; b_T(E_1,t) \, b_T(E_2,r)\, e^{\alpha E_1} \, \frac{G_\epsilon (E_1-E_2)}{\lambda }, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Σtrϵλ=∫dE1dE2bT(E1,t)bT(E2,r)eαE1Gϵ(E1-E2)λ,149\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} & \frac{F^{\epsilon }_t(\omega )}{\lambda } = \int \textrm{d}E \, b_T(t,E) \, e^{\alpha E} \; \frac{G_\epsilon (E-\omega )}{\lambda }. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Ftϵ(ω)λ=∫dEbT(t,E)eαEGϵ(E-ω)λ.The resulting expression for the coefficients of Eq. (145) is150\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \vec {g}^{\textrm{GP}}(\epsilon ; \omega ) = \vec {F}^{\, T}(\omega ) \, \frac{1}{\Sigma ^\epsilon + \lambda \, C_Y} \;. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}g→GP(ϵ;ω)=F→T(ω)1Σϵ+λCY.It is clear that λλ is parametrizing the cut-off on the low modes of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\Sigma ^\epsilon$$\end{document}Σϵ, thus introducing a bias. When λλ becomes larger the role of the regularizing term, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY, is enhanced, and the coefficients \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$g_\tau$$\end{document}gτ become increasingly smaller. At smaller values of λλ, the bias decreases, but it cannot be eliminated. Its dependence must be, therefore, addressed, and its effect quantified and controlled. The role of such parameter is extensively discussed in the context of Backus–Gilbert methods, and in particular in its formulation of Ref. [9], where it is usually prescribed to choose λλ, such that the prediction for the spectral density is stable within statistical noise [14–16] upon variations of λλ. From this “stability analysis”, the bias is assumed to be absorbed into the statistical error, a procedure that has been validated numerically; see for instance Refs. [14, 16, 17]. In the context of GPs, on the other hand, the hyperparameters (including λλ) are selected, so that the resulting probability of observing the data151\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {G} \left[ \vec {C}^{\, \mathrm obs} - \vec {C}^{\; \mathrm{prior}} ; \, \Sigma + C_Y \right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}GC→obs-C→prior;Σ+CY,is maximized [13, 18].5 Equivalently, one minimizes the “negative logarithmic likelihood” (NLL)152\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\tau _{\textrm{max}}}{2}\, \text {Log} (2\pi ) +\frac{1}{2} \, \text {Log}\, \text {det} \left( \Sigma + \text {Cov}_d \right) +\frac{1}{2} \, \left(\vec {C}^{\, \mathrm obs}- \vec {C}^{\; \mathrm{prior}}\right) \frac{1}{\Sigma +\text {Cov}_d} \left(\vec {C}^{\, \mathrm obs}- \vec {C}^{\; \mathrm{prior}}\right). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}τmax2Log(2π)+12LogdetΣ+Covd+12C→obs-C→prior1Σ+CovdC→obs-C→prior.

In this setting, the fate of the bias introduced by λλ could be considered opaque. We, therefore, assess whether porting the “stability analysis” that is carried in Ref. [9] into the Bayesian setup, can add insights in this regard. In Fig. 4, we show how the choice of λλ affects the spectral reconstruction (left panel)6 at a specific energy. The corresponding values of the NLL are shown in the right panel of the same figure, with the minimum value highlighted. For large values of λλ, the smeared spectral density changes considerably, showing a large dependence on the prior. The corresponding values of the NLL are large. Remarkably, as the NLL approaches its minimum, the dependence on the prior softens. The horizontal band in the left panel of Fig. 4 is obtained at the value of λλ that minimizes the NLL, which is flagged by a star in the right panel. This result suggests that the treatment of the bias might not be drastically different in these two cases, despite the very different approaches.Fig. 4Combination of the stability analysis used (left) and a scan of the NLL (right). The smeared density shown in the left panel is obtained at a specific energy, and is evaluated from GPs, i.e., from Eqs. (143) and (144) for the central value and the error, respectively. The prior is the modified Gaussian of Eq. (147), with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon \simeq m_0$$\end{document}ϵ≃m0, the latter being the ground state of the channel. We only display value corresponding to α =0α=0, because these correspond to a systematically smaller NLL. The purpose of this figure is to show that the treatment of the parameter λλ from Ref. [9] and GPs can lead to compatible results. Details about the lattice data used for this example are found in the main text

The smeared density shown in the left panel is obtained at a specific energy, and is evaluated from GPs, i.e., from Eqs. (143) and (144) for the central value and the error, respectively. Another important remark, already stated in the previous section, is that the value obtained at the minimum of the NLL (horizontal band in Fig. 4) has to be interpreted and understood as a spectral density that is smeared with the appropriate smearing kernel, which is not known a priori in the Bayesian formulation given in this section. In this respect, this method is closer to the original proposal of Backus and Gilbert rather than the modification of Ref. [9]. For this reason, we find it instructive to show, in the left panel of Fig. 5, an example of the smearing kernel at a specific energy (\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega = 3.7 m_\pi$$\end{document}ω=3.7mπ) obtained with the Bayesian setup (orange), as opposed to the smearing kernel obtained from the method (in blue) of Ref. [9] (HLT in short) that will be described in the next section. The Bayesian kernel is not known a priori: its behavior is constrained by the covariance of the prior, which can produce a smoother or more rapidly changing function. In the example of Fig. 5, the prior is defined in Eq. 147, with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon = 0.75 m_\pi$$\end{document}ϵ=0.75mπ. The output thus features, around \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\omega = 3.7 m_\pi$$\end{document}ω=3.7mπ, oscillation that have roughly a wavelength of εϵ. In the right panel of the same figure, the smeared spectral density obtained with the two methods is also displayed. In this example, we have used synthetic data without any statistical noise, in order to showcase what each method does in the ideal limit of exact data. The input correlator contains a single state with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E/m_\pi = 3.7$$\end{document}E/mπ=3.7.Fig. 5Left panel: examples of the function smearing the spectral density at the energy \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$E = 3.7 m_\pi$$\end{document}E=3.7mπ in the Bayesian setup (orange) and from the HLT procedure (blue) using exact data. The latter targets a Gaussian kernel with a width of approximately 0.75 in units of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$m_\pi$$\end{document}mπ, which is reconstructed with great precision given the lack of uncertainties on the input data. For the Bayesian calculation, we use the same Gaussian function as a prior, but we have less control on the output function, which in this case features oscillations with a length scale determined by the prior. The right panel displays the reconstructed smeared spectral densities from the same data. This example uses \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$t_{\textrm{max}}=32$$\end{document}tmax=32 data points

Neural networks

Neural networks basics

In the context of PDF fitting, the NNPDF collaboration has pioneered the use of neural networks as a flexible parametrization of PDFs, which allows to reduce the bias introduced by the choice of a specific functional form. The NNPDF approach is based on the idea of using a large number of parameters (the weights and biases of the neural network) to fit the data, while monitoring the fitting process in order to prevent overfitting, see e.g. Ref. [2] for details. In this language, the ensemble of NN replicas at initialization defines the prior probability distribution of the model, and the training process is used to update the probability distribution in light of the data, thus defining the posterior distribution. Note that, while we have prior and posterior distributions, the inference is not performed according to Bayes’ theorem.

In these section, we briefly recall some basic notions about neural networks, and develop a statistical field theory description of the behavior of neural networks at initialization, which will be the foundation for the discussion of the training process in the next section.

A neural network is one way of parametrizing a function, f(x), using interconnected layers of computing units, which are called neurons by analogy with the biological neurons. We are going to focus here on the simplest type of neural network, which is the feed-forward neural network, where the information flows in one direction from the input layer, identified by ℓ =0ℓ=0 to the output layer, identified by ℓ =Lℓ=L. The neuron in each layer is labeled by an index i, and the number of neurons in layer ℓℓ is denoted by \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_\ell$$\end{document}nℓ. The input of the network is x, which is a vector of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0$$\end{document}n0, and the output is f(x), which is a vector of dimension \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_L$$\end{document}nL. If we are parametrizing a function that returns a scalar, then \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_L=1$$\end{document}nL=1. At each neuron, we associated a so-called pre-activation function, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{(\ell )}_i(x)$$\end{document}ϕi(ℓ)(x). The indices ℓℓ and i identify the neuron, and we explicitly wrote the dependence on the input x. The output of the neuron is obtained by applying the activation function ρρ to the pre-activation153\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \rho ^{(\ell )}_i(x) = \rho \left( \phi ^{(\ell )}_i(x) \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ρi(ℓ)(x)=ρϕi(ℓ)(x).The pre-activation function are recursively defined154\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi ^{(\ell )}_i(x) = \sum _{j=1}^{n_{\ell -1}} W^{(\ell )}_{ij} \, \rho ^{(\ell -1)}_j(x) + b^{(\ell )}_i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕi(ℓ)(x)=∑j=1nℓ-1Wij(ℓ)ρj(ℓ-1)(x)+bi(ℓ),with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{(0)}_i(x) = x_i$$\end{document}ϕi(0)(x)=xi. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{(\ell )}$$\end{document}W(ℓ) are the weight matrices, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^{(l)}$$\end{document}b(l) are the bias vectors. For each input x, the recursion relations above allow us to compute the pre-activation and the output of each neuron, and ultimately the output of the network, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_i(x)=\phi ^{(L)}_i(x)$$\end{document}fi(x)=ϕi(L)(x).

The weights and biases are the parameters of the neural network, they are initialized according to some prescription and they are then determined by the training process. Neural networks provide a flexible parametrization of functions and are known to be universal approximators for sufficiently large number of parameters. They were originally introduced for PDF fitting precisely in order to avoid any bias introduced by a fixed functional form with insufficient degrees of freedom.

Typically, we consider a discrete set of input points, labeled by an index αα as discussed in the previous sections. The input points are therefore \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_\alpha$$\end{document}xα. In order to simplify the notation, we introduce155\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i\alpha } = \phi ^{(\ell )}_i(x_\alpha ), \qquad \rho ^{(\ell )}_{i\alpha } = \rho ^{(\ell )}_i(x_\alpha ). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕiα(ℓ)=ϕi(ℓ)(xα),ρiα(ℓ)=ρi(ℓ)(xα).It is important to distinguish between the index αα, which labels the input points, and the index i, which labels the neurons. For each αα, the input x could be a vector, whose components are labeled by the index \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$i=1, \ldots , n_0$$\end{document}i=1,…,n0. A typical example of an NN is shown in Fig. 6. Each dot in the figure represents a neuron, so that for each dot, we have a pre-activation \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{(\ell )}_{i\alpha }$$\end{document}ϕiα(ℓ) and an output \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{(\ell )}_{i\alpha }$$\end{document}ρiα(ℓ). The actual values of the pre-activation and the output depend on the input \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_\alpha$$\end{document}xα, which is not explicitly shown in the figure. The connections between the dots represent the weights \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$W^{(\ell )}_{ij}$$\end{document}Wij(ℓ), and the bias \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$b^{(\ell )}_i$$\end{document}bi(ℓ) is associated with each neuron. The input layer is in green on the left, the output layer is in red on the right, and the hidden layers are in blue. Note that the NN shown in the figure has two neurons in the input layer. Having two neurons in the input layer allows us to use both x and łog xlogx as inputs, which enhances the sensitivity of the NN to the small-x region.Fig. 6A feed-forward neural network with L=3L=3 layers and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0=2$$\end{document}n0=2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1=25$$\end{document}n1=25, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_2=20$$\end{document}n2=20, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_3=8$$\end{document}n3=8. The input layer is in green on the left, the output layer is in red on the right, and the hidden layers are in blue. This is the actual architecture of the neural network used by the NNPDF collaboration for PDF fitting; see Ref. [2]

A feed-forward neural network with L=3L=3 layers and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_0=2$$\end{document}n0=2, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_1=25$$\end{document}n1=25, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_2=20$$\end{document}n2=20, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_3=8$$\end{document}n3=8. The input layer is in green on the left, the output layer is in red on the right, and the hidden layers are in blue. This is the actual architecture of the neural network used by the NNPDF collaboration for PDF fitting; see Ref. [2]

Neural networks at initialization

The weights and biases of the NN are initialized as independent Gaussian variables156\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} W^{(\ell )}_{ij} \sim \mathcal {N}\left( 0, \frac{C_W}{n_{\ell -1 }}\right) , \qquad b^{(\ell )}_i \sim \mathcal {N}(0, C_b). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Wij(ℓ)∼N0,CWnℓ-1,bi(ℓ)∼N(0,Cb).Both distributions have zero mean. The variance of the weights in layer ℓℓ is scaled by the number of neurons in the previous layer, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{\ell -1}$$\end{document}nℓ-1. This is a common choice, sometimes referred to as the Glorot initialization [19]. By inspecting the recursion relation in Eq. 154, it is clear that the scaling of the variance guarantees that the pre-activations have a finite limit when the number of neurons in the previous layer becomes large. The average over the probability distributions in Eq. 156 yields157\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E}[W^{(\ell )}_{ij}] = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[Wij(ℓ)]=0,158\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E}[b^{(\ell )}_i] = 0, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[bi(ℓ)]=0,159\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E}[W^{(\ell )}_{ij} W^{(\ell )}_{i'j'}] = \delta _{ii'}\delta _{jj'} \frac{C_W}{n_{\ell -1}}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[Wij(ℓ)Wi′j′(ℓ)]=δii′δjj′CWnℓ-1,160\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\mathbb {E}[b^{(\ell )}_i b^{(\ell )}_{i'}] = \delta _{ii'} \, C_b, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}E[bi(ℓ)b′(ℓ)]=δii′Cb,and all other correlations—in particular correlations between different layers—vanish. The \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{(0)}$$\end{document}ϕ(0) are given by the values of the input, and therefore are deterministic quantities. All other pre-activations are stochastic variables and we are interested in their joint probability distribution161\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P\left( \phi ^{(L)} \ldots \phi ^{(1)}\right) = P\left( \phi ^{(L)}|\phi ^{(L-1)}\right) \ldots P\left( \phi ^{(1)}|\phi ^{(0)}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pϕ(L)…ϕ(1)=Pϕ(L)|ϕ(L-1)…Pϕ(1)|ϕ(0).The following derivations are based on the recent review in Ref. [20].

A forward recursion relation.

The probability distribution of the pre-activations in layer ℓℓ obeys a forward recursion relation, which allows to compute it from the probability distribution of the pre-activations in layer ℓ -1ℓ-1162\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P\left( \phi ^{(\ell +1)} | \phi ^{(0)}\right) = \int \textrm{d}\phi ^{(\ell )} \, P\left( \phi ^{(\ell +1)} | \phi ^{(\ell )}\right) P\left( \phi ^{(\ell )} | \phi ^{(0)}\right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pϕ(ℓ+1)|ϕ(0)=∫dϕ(ℓ)Pϕ(ℓ+1)|ϕ(ℓ)Pϕ(ℓ)|ϕ(0).The conditional probability distribution \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$P\left( \phi ^{(\ell +1)} | \phi ^{(\ell )}\right)$$\end{document}Pϕ(ℓ+1)|ϕ(ℓ) can be computed by integrating over the weights and biases163\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P\left( \phi ^{(\ell +1)}|\phi ^{(\ell )}\right)&= \int \prod _{i,j} \textrm{d}W^{(\ell +1)}_{ij} \prod _i \textrm{d}b^{(\ell +1)}_i \, p\left( W^{(\ell +1)}\right) p\left( b^{(\ell +1)}\right) \, \nonumber \\&\qquad \times \ \prod _i \delta \left( \phi ^{(\ell +1)}_i - \sum _j W^{(\ell +1)}_{ij} \rho ^{(\ell )}_j - b^{(\ell +1)}_i \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pϕ(ℓ+1)|ϕ(ℓ)=∫∏i,jdWij(ℓ+1)∏idbi(ℓ+1)pW(ℓ+1)pb(ℓ+1)×∏iδϕi(ℓ+1)-∑jWij(ℓ+1)ρj(ℓ)-bi(ℓ+1),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\left( W^{(\ell +1)}\right)$$\end{document}pW(ℓ+1) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$p\left( b^{(\ell +1)}\right)$$\end{document}pb(ℓ+1) are the probability distributions of the weights and biases in layer ℓ +1ℓ+1 given in Eq. 156. The delta functions in the last line enforce the definition of the pre-activations in terms of the weights, biases, and activations in the previous layer. For fixed values of the pre-activations in layer ℓℓ, the pre-activations in layer ℓ +1ℓ+1 are linear combinations of the weights and biases, and therefore, their probability distribution is Gaussian164\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P\left( \phi ^{(\ell +1)}|\phi ^{(\ell )}\right) = \frac{1}{\left| 2\pi \hat{G}^{(\ell +1)}\right| ^{n_{\ell +1}/2}} \, \exp \left( -\frac{1}{2} \phi ^{(\ell +1)}_{\alpha _1} \cdot \phi ^{(\ell +1)}_{\alpha _2} \left( \hat{G}^{(\ell +1)}\right) ^{-1}_{\alpha _1 \alpha _2} \right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pϕ(ℓ+1)|ϕ(ℓ)=12πG^(ℓ+1)nℓ+1/2exp-12ϕ1(ℓ+1)·ϕ2(ℓ+1)G^(ℓ+1)α1α2-1,where the scalar product indicates the contraction of the neuron indices, and we introduced the propagator165\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \hat{G}^{(\ell +1)}_{\alpha _1 \alpha _2} = C_b + C_W \, \frac{1}{n_\ell } \rho ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1} \cdot \rho ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}G^α1α2(ℓ+1)=Cb+CW1nℓρ1(ℓ)·ρ2(ℓ).The factor of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/n_\ell$$\end{document}1/nℓ in Eq. 165 comes from the scaling of the variance of the weights in Eq. 156. The scalar product between the outputs \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\rho ^{(\ell )}$$\end{document}ρ(ℓ) involves a sum over the neuron indices, and therefore, it is of order \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_\ell$$\end{document}nℓ; the propagator has a finite limit when the number of neurons in layer ℓℓ becomes large. Clearly, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\hat{G}^{(\ell +1)}$$\end{document}G^(ℓ+1) depends on the pre-activations \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\phi ^{(\ell )}$$\end{document}ϕ(ℓ).

Exercise 6

Show that the correlators of the pre-activations in any layer ℓℓ are symmetric under rotations in the space of neurons, i.e.166\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\left[ R_{i_1j_1} \phi ^{(\ell )}_{j_1 \alpha _1} \ldots R_{i_nj_n} \phi ^{(\ell )}_{j_n \alpha _n} \right] = \mathbb {E}\left[ \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i_1 \alpha _1} \ldots \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i_n \alpha _n} \right] , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ERi1j1ϕj1α1(ℓ)…Rinjnϕjnαn(ℓ)=Eϕi1α1(ℓ)…ϕinαn(ℓ),where R is an orthogonal matrix in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {SO}(n_{\ell })$$\end{document}SO(nℓ). Equation(166) implies that the probability distribution is also invariant under rotations, and therefore, it can only be a function of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\text {SO}(n_{\ell })$$\end{document}SO(nℓ) invariants, i.e. it can only depend on the scalar product of the pre-activations.

In the first hidden layer, ℓ = 1ℓ=1, the pre-activations are linear combinations of the weights and biases167\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \phi ^{(1)}_{i\alpha } = \sum _j W^{(1)}_{ij} \rho \left( x_{\alpha j}\right) + b^{(1)}_i, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϕiα(1)=∑jWij(1)ρxαj+bi(1),and therefore, they are Gaussian variables, with zero mean and covariance168\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathbb {E}\left[ \phi ^{(1)}_{i\alpha } \phi ^{(1)}_{i'\alpha '} \right] = \delta _{ii'} \left( C_b + C_W \, \frac{1}{n_0} \sum _j \rho \left( x_{\alpha j}\right) \rho \left( x_{\alpha ' j}\right) \right) . \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Eϕiα(1)ϕi′α′(1)=δii′Cb+CW1n0∑jρxαjρxα′j.One can readily check, by writing down the explicit expression, that the distributions are no longer Gaussian as soon as we move to ℓ> 1ℓ>1.

Effective theory approach.

It is useful to rewrite the recursion relation in (162) by introducing an effective action, \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$S^{(\ell )}$$\end{document}S(ℓ), defined by169\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} P\left( \phi ^{(\ell )} | \phi ^{(0)}\right) = \frac{1}{Z^{(\ell )}} \, e^{-S^{(\ell )}(\phi ^{(\ell )})}, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Pϕ(ℓ)|ϕ(0)=1Z(ℓ)e-S(ℓ)(ϕ(ℓ)),where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$Z^{(\ell )}$$\end{document}Z(ℓ) is a normalization factor. The recursion relation in Eq. 162 can be rewritten as170\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{e^{-S^{(\ell +1)}(\phi ^{(\ell +1)})}}{Z^{(\ell +1)}} = \int \textrm{d}\phi ^{(\ell )} \, \frac{e^{-S^{(\ell )}(\phi ^{(\ell )})}}{Z^{(\ell )}} \, \frac{\exp \left( -\frac{1}{2} \phi ^{(\ell +1)}_{\alpha _1} \cdot \phi ^{(\ell +1)}_{\alpha _2} \left( \hat{G}^{(\ell +1)}\right) ^{-1}_{\alpha _1 \alpha _2} \right) }{\left| 2\pi \hat{G}^{(\ell +1)}\right| ^{n_{\ell +1}/2}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}e-S(ℓ+1)(ϕ(ℓ+1))Z(ℓ+1)=∫dϕ(ℓ)e-S(ℓ)(ϕ(ℓ))Z(ℓ)exp-12ϕ1(ℓ+1)·ϕ2(ℓ+1)G^(ℓ+1)α1α2-12πG^(ℓ+1)nℓ+1/2.In order to extract further information from the recursion relation, we need to parametrize the effective action. The most general form of the effective action is171\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} S^{(\ell )}(\phi ^{(\ell )}) = \frac{1}{2}\, \gamma ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1 \alpha _2} \, \left( \phi ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1} \cdot \phi ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _2}\right) + \frac{1}{8 n_{\ell -1}} \, \gamma ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1 \alpha _2; \alpha _3 \alpha _4} \, \left( \phi ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1} \cdot \phi ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _2} \right) \left( \phi ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _3} \cdot \phi ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _4} \right) + \cdots , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}S(ℓ)(ϕ(ℓ))=12γα1α2(ℓ)ϕ1(ℓ)·ϕ2(ℓ)+18nℓ-1γα1α2;α3α4(ℓ)ϕ1(ℓ)·ϕ2(ℓ)ϕ3(ℓ)·ϕ4(ℓ)+⋯,where we introduced the couplings \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1 \alpha _2}$$\end{document}γα1α2(ℓ) and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\gamma ^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1 \alpha _2; \alpha _3 \alpha _4}$$\end{document}γα1α2;α3α4(ℓ), which are functions of the layer ℓℓ and of the input points. Note that because of symmetry reasons, the effective action can only depend on the scalar product of the pre-activations. The factors in the action reflect the symmetries of each term, and the scaling with the number of neurons in the previous layer is chosen, so that the couplings have a finite limit when \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$n_{\ell -1}$$\end{document}nℓ-1 becomes large. We have omitted higher order terms in the effective action; they involve higher powers of the scalar product of the pre-activations, and they are suppressed by higher powers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/n_{\ell -1}$$\end{document}1/nℓ-1.

At leading order, the second and fourth cumulants are respectively172\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\langle \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i_1,\alpha _1} \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i_2,\alpha _2}\rangle = \delta _{i_1 i_2} K^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1\alpha _2} + O(1/n_{\ell -1}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ϕi1,α1(ℓ)ϕi2,α2(ℓ)⟩=δi1i2Kα1α2(ℓ)+O(1/nℓ-1),173\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned}&\langle \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i_1,\alpha _1} \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i_2,\alpha _2} \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i_3,\alpha _3} \phi ^{(\ell )}_{i_4,\alpha _4}\rangle _c = O(1/n_{\ell -1}), \end{aligned}$$\end{document}⟨ϕi1,α1(ℓ)ϕi2,α2(ℓ)ϕi3,α3(ℓ)ϕi4,α4(ℓ)⟩c=O(1/nℓ-1),where7174\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} K^{(\ell )}_{\alpha _1\alpha _2} = \left( \gamma ^{(\ell )}\right) ^{-1}_{\alpha _1\alpha _2}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Kα1α2(ℓ)=γ(ℓ)α1α2-1.

Exercise 7

Derive the Feynman rules for the effective theory defined by the action in Eq. (171), and use them to compute the second and fourth cumulants of the pre-activations at leading order in \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/n_{\ell -1}$$\end{document}1/nℓ-1, thus showing that they are given by the expressions in Eqs. The “evolution” of the couplings as we go deep in the NN, i.e., the dependence of the couplings on ℓℓ, is governed by Renormalization Group (RG) equations, which preserve the power counting in powers of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$1/n_{\ell }$$\end{document}1/nℓ. As a consequence of the symmetry of the probability distribution, the mean value of the fields at initialization needs to vanish, while their variance at each point \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$x_\alpha$$\end{document}xα is given by the diagonal matrix elements of \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$K^{(\ell )}$$\end{document}K(ℓ). In Fig. 9, the expected distribution is compared against the empirical distribution of output fields for a selected value of x, using two ensembles of replicas with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{{\textrm{rep}}}=100$$\end{document}Nrep=100 and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$N_{{\textrm{rep}}}=1000$$\end{document}Nrep=1000, respectively. Inspecting the figures, we conclude that the recursion formula, Eq. (176), accurately reproduces the output distribution of the NNPDF networks at initialization, provided that a sufficiently large ensemble of replicas is used to sample the distribution. Training neural networks

We have summarized some properties of the neural network at initialization, showing that the normal Glorot initialization leads to a prior distribution that is a Gaussian processes with small corrections. In the final part of these lectures, we analyze the training dynamics of the neural network, with the aim of understanding how the process of training yields the posterior distribution of the solution to the inverse problem. We follow very closely the presentation of Ref. [21].

In the context of machine learning, specifically when dealing with neural networks, optimization is an iterative algorithm that updates the parameters of the network in order to minimize a figure of merit defined appropriately. The direction toward the minimum is defined by the gradient of the figure of merit. Due to the large number of parameters that characterize a neural network, and the complex functional form induced by the recursive definition of the network, the figure of merit (also known as error function, loss function, or simply loss) is a non-convex high-dimensional function of the parameters, leading to numerical challenges in the minimization task. In addition, in order to avoid over- and under-learning, training algorithms require a stopping criterion, which specifies the optimal condition to end the training process. While these algorithms have achieved remarkable empirical success, a theoretical understanding of the optimization process remains elusive. In these lectures, we want to focus on understanding the dynamics, without being bogged down by unnecessary technical details; therefore, we work with the simplest gradient method, i.e., Gradient Descent (GD).

We work out in detail one specific example, in the context of PDF determinations, where we consider a reduced dataset for which predictions can be computed using just one flavor combination of the PDFs, so that the theoretical prediction for the data remains simply180\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} T_I[f] = \int \textrm{d}x\, C_{I}(x) f(x). \end{aligned}$$\end{document}TI[f]=∫dxCI(x)f(x).The details of the dataset and the definition of the flavor combination are not relevant for these lectures, and the interested reader can find more details in Ref. [21].

Training in functional space

For analytical tractability, GD is described as a continuous flow of the parameters θθ in training time t along the negative gradient of the loss function \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}$$\end{document}L. For sufficiently small learning rates ηη, this continuous flow approximates the discrete GD trajectory in parameter space, as extensively discussed in Ref. [22]. The continuous Gradient Flow (GF) is then given by181\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \frac{\textrm{d}}{\textrm{d}t}&\theta _{t,\mu } = -\nabla _\mu \mathcal {L}_t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ddtθt,μ=-∇μLt,where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\theta _{t,\mu }$$\end{document}θt,μ and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\mathcal {L}_t$$\end{document}Lt identify, respectively, the parameters and the loss function at training time t. We distinguish between the continuous training time t and the discrete epochs of GD, the latter denoted using the capital letter T. For the purpose of this lecture, the two are related through a constant learning rate, t = η Tt=ηT with \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\eta = 10^{-5}$$\end{document}η=10-5, and we will use them interchangeably.

We focus here on quadratic loss functions that are obtained as the negative logarithm of Gaussian data distributions around their theoretical predictions; using the notation introduced in Sect. 2, the loss function is given by182\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \mathcal {L}_t = \frac{1}{2} \left( Y - T[f_t]\right) ^T C_Y^{-1} \left( Y - T[f_t]\right) , \end{aligned}$$\end{document}Lt=12Y-T[ft]TCY-1Y-T[ft],where \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_t$$\end{document}ft is the output of the network at training time t, obtained from the time-dependence of the internal parameters, and \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$C_Y$$\end{document}CY is the covariance of the data. Note that the loss function at training time t is computed using the theoretical prediction \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$T[f_t]$$\end{document}T[ft], computed using a grid of points for \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$f_t$$\end{document}ft at training time t. For a quadratic loss, the gradient is183\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \nabla _\mu \mathcal {L}_t = - \left( \nabla _\mu f_t\right) ^T \left( \frac{\partial T}{\partial f}\right) _t C_Y^{-1} \epsilon _t, \end{aligned}$$\end{document}∇μLt=-∇μftT∂T∂ftCY-1ϵt,where writing explicitly the data index184\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\begin{aligned} \epsilon _{t,I} = Y_I - T_I[f_t], \quad I=1, \ldots , N_{\textrm{dat}}. \end{aligned}$$\end{document}ϵt,I=YI-TI[ft],I=1,…,Ndat.For the specific case of a quadratic loss function, the gradient is proportional to \documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym} \usepackage{amsfonts} \usepackage{amssymb} \usepackage{amsbsy} \usepackage{mathrsfs} \usepackage{upgreek} \setlength{\oddsidemargin}{-69pt} \begin{document}$$\epsilon _t$$\end{document}ϵt, which is the difference between the theoretical prediction and the data at training time t. If at some point during the training the theoretical predictions reproduce all the data, the training process ends.

A further simplification is obtained in the case of data that depend linearly on the unknown function f. In this case, the Jacobian of the theoretical prediction with respect to the fields is independent of the training time185\documentclass[12pt]{minimal} \usepackage{amsmath} \usepackage{wasysym}
